MHC II - TCR interaction

CD40 - CD40L interaction

Secretion of IL-21

→ B cell proliferation and differentiation, induce the action of AID enzyme

Immunoglobulin Heavy Chain class switching (isotype switching)

→ AID enzyme: Activation Induced Deaminase

Class switching

Affinity maturation (through somatic hypermutation)

Cuts in the constant region, then join to the same VDL (variable) region → Allows class switching of immunoglobulins

Selection of high affinity B cells

B cell activation by protein antigen & helper T cells

Clonal expansion, somatic hypermutation (of the variable region)

→ Many B cells with different affinities to antigen

B cells will then compete to:

Bind to antigens follicular dendritic cells (FDCs)

Present antigen to helper T cells

→ Fail to recognize Ag on FDCs or interact with CD4+ T cells

→ Die → High affinity B cell selection

Following immunization, serum Ab is detectable for a long time, but there are no plasma cells in lymph nodes or spleen. Which cells are making the antibody and where are they?

Plasma cells are produced during germinal center (GC) reaction

Migrate to bone marrow (and mucosal tissues)

Survive there for years (long-lived plasma cells)

→ Release circulating IgGs, provides long-term protection

Follicular Helper T cells (Tfh)

Express chemokine receptor CXCR5

→ Migrate to lymphoid follicles

→ Help B cells with:

Class switching

Affinity maturation

Characteristics of follicular helper T cells:

Surface CXCR5, ICOS

Transcription factor: BCL-6

Secrete cytokines: IL-21 + IL-4 / IFN-γ

Germinal Center Reaction

Location: Lymph nodes & Spleen (inside B cell follicles)

Germinal center: site of humoral response development

Responses developed during the GC reaction include:

Isotype switching

Affinity maturation

Long-lived plasma cells

Memory B cells

Driven by: Follicular helper T cells

→ Maximize the GC reaction during effective vaccine development

Effector functions of antibodies:

Neutralize microbes and toxins

Opsonize antigens → Phagocytosis by other immune cells

Antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity (NK cells release toxic molecules)

Complement activation:

Lysis of microbes

Opsonization with complement fragments

Trigger inflammation

Diseases of the immune system

Excessive, uncontrolled immune responses

Response against self-antigens → Autoimmune diseases

Immune system defect (HIV, AIDS, …)

Immunological tolerance

Definition:

Unresponsiveness to an antigen

Induced by exposure of lymphocytes to that antigen

Is antigen-specific

Significance:

All individuals are tolerant of their own antigens (self-tolerance) → Prevent autoimmunity

Prevent immune responses to:

Self antigens

Commensal microbes

Fetal antigens

Therapeutic potential → Exploited for autoimmune & allergy treatment (by inducing tolerance)

Self-tolerance: Central Tolerance & Peripheral Tolerance

Central tolerance:

When? → During lymphocyte maturation

问Where?
答

Thymus (T cells)

Bone marrow (B cells)

→ Strong recognition of self antigen leads to deletion (by apoptosis): NEGATIVE SELECTION

Peripheral tolerance:

When? → After lymphocytes have matured

Where? → In peripheral tissues (lymph nodes, spleen, tissues)

→ If a mature lymphocyte strongly recognizes self antigens, its function is inhibited

Consequences of self antigen recognition

Strong affinity → Deletion: Negative selection

Moderate affinity → Development of regulatory T cells (Tregs) → Maintain peripheral tolerance

→ Both mechanisms prevent autoimmunity

How self antigens are expressed in the thymus

mTECs (Medullary Thymic Epithelial Cells):

Mimic different peripheral tissues (skin, lung, liver, intestine,...)

Express antigens of these cells (self antigens)

AIRE (Autoimmune Regulator):

Transcription factor expressed in mTECs

Induce transcription of organ-specific genes

→ Produce proteins that are normally restricted to peripheral tissues

Peripheral Tolerance

Happen after T cells have matured and left the thymus

→ Prevent reaction of mature T cells against self-antigen

Related to signal 1 and 2 of T cell activation:

Mature T cells + Immunogenic Antigen

2 signals:

Signal 1: Antigen (peptide) + HLA

Signal 2: Costimulation CD80 - CD28 interaction

→ Effector & Memory T cells

Mature T cells + Tolerogenic Antigen

Only 1 signal (Antigen + HLA)

→ Tolerance:

Functional inactivation

Cell death

Increased sensitivity to suppression

Regulatory T cells

Definition:

A special subset of Helper T cells

Suppress immune responses & Maintain tolerance to self-antigens

Expression of:

Master transcription factor: FOXP3

High CD25 (IL-2Rα)

CTLA-4

Origin: Naive CD4+ T cells recognize self antigen (mild affinity)

In the thymus (primary lymphoid organ)

In the lymph node (secondary lymphoid organ)

Functions:

Suppress the immune system

Prevent auto-immunity

How Tregs suppress the immune system & prevent autoimmunity:

CTLA-4 inhibition of APCs → Inhibit costimulatory signal (CD80/86 - CD28 → CD80/86 - CTLA-4)

Secrete anti-inflammatory cytokines

Suppression of effector T cell proliferation

Non-immune functions of tissue Tregs

Tregs in adipose tissue → Regulate lipid metabolism

Tregs in muscle and other tissues → Produce growth factors that promote repair (in trauma, infections & degenerative diseases)

Tregs in skin tissue → Stimulate cycling & differentiation of hair follicle stem cells

The significance of recent advances

Solid foundation of basic principles

Improve understanding of disease mechanisms (often based on animal models)

Development of novel therapies

Appreciate the role of the immune system in non-immune diseases (cardio-immunology, lactating breast, …)

MUCOSAL IMMUNOLOGY

Definition of mucosal immunology

The study of immune responses that occur at mucosal membranes (of the intestines, the urogenital tract & the respiratory system)

Mucous membranes are in constant contact with microorganisms, food & inhaled antigens

Human respiratory tract

Anatomic components:

Upper respiratory tract:

Nasal cavity

Pharynx

Larynx

Lower respiratory tract:

Conducting airway: trachea & bronchi

Respiratory zone: alveoli

→ Air is distributed through the lung lobes in a tree-like branched conducting system

Micro level: the epithelium of the conducting airways is lined with different cell types → Physical & Chemical barriers to infection

Ciliated cells → Clearance of mucus

Goblet cells → Secretion of mucus

Club (Clara) cells → secretion of protective glycosaminoglycans

Basal cells → progenitor cells

Other cells: Tuft cells, ionocytes, pulmonary endocrine cells (PNECs)

Anatomy and physiology of the respiratory system

Immunological anatomy of lung

Homeostasis: a state of balance

Maintain homeostasis

Lung epithelium: Physical barrier → Protect lung tissue from bacteria, viruses, allergies & harmful substances

Harmonious coordination of immune pathways, which involves the active participation of:

Alveolar epithelial cells (AECs):

Essential in airway defence mechanisms

Two types: AEC I & AEC II

AEC II: more immunologically active

Function:

Produce cytokines & chemokines

→ Immune cell activation & differentiation

APCs for specific T cells

Macrophages (lower respiratory tract macrophages)

Alveolar macrophages (AMs):

In the alveoli

Innately suppressive

Maintain immunological homeostasis & host defense

Non-specific defences

Interstitial macrophages (IMs):

In the interstitium

Regulatory function within lung tissue

Adaptive immune response

Cells involved in primary immune response of the lung

Airway epithelial cells:

Barrier function

Maintain fluid balance

Regulate mucus & surfactant production

Initiate immune response (cytokine, chemokine, GF production)

Neutrophils:

Link between innate & adaptive immunity

Clearance of pathogens & cell debris

Involved in pathogenesis of lung diseases

Alveolar macrophages:

Phagocytic & antigen presenting cells

First-line defence against pollutants & pathogens

Specialized in detecting environmental signals

Communicate with other cells to maintain lung homeostasis

Innate lymphoid cells (ILCs):

Lack antigen-specific receptors

Protect the lung by producing IFN‐γ, IL-5, IL-13, IL-17 & IL-22

Induce airway hyperinflammation

Promote allergic inflammation

Block extracellular bacterial infections

Mucosal-Associated Invariant T cells (MAIT):

Maintain immune homeostasis

Innate immunity to pathogens

Directly associated with pathogenesis of lung diseases

Involved in immune response against SARS-CoV-2

Dendritic cells:

Initiate adaptive immune response

Initiate inflammation

Lung infection: COVID-19

The viral spike (S) protein binds to the angiotensin-converting enzyme 2 (ACE-2) receptor on host cell surface

Type 2 transmembrane serine protease (TMPRSS2): host cell protease → Cleave both ACE-2 receptor & spike protein

→ Activate spike protein

Activated spike protein enables fusion & viral entry into the cell (TMPRSS2 increases the efficiency of viral uptake)

Transmission of COVID-19

COVID-19 infects cells that co-express ACE2 and TMPRSS2, which are found mainly in the lung and olfactory epithelium, among other tissues.

This widespread expression explains both respiratory multi-organ involvement of SARS-CoV-2.

ACE2 expression is highest in nasal goblet and ciliated epithelial cells, moderate in lower airway secretory/ciliated cells, and lower but functionally important in alveolar type II cells.

This distribution explains efficient viral entry through the nose and severe lung involvement in advanced COVID-19.

Expression profile of ACE-2 and TMPRSS2 is age-dependent

Pediatric lungs express significantly lower levels of ACE2 and TMPRSS2, especially in alveolar and ciliated epithelial cells.

This reduced receptor availability likely contributes to reduced susceptibility and milder COVID-19 in children.

Moderate & Severe COVID-19 cases

Moderate: Early Type-I IFN response → Antiviral immune response that control the viral load → Controlled level of inflammatory cytokines → Recruitment of NK cells, T cells & antibody production by B cells → Viral clearance

Severe: Delayed, weaker antiviral Type-I IFN response → High viral load → Alarmingly high level of inflammatory cytokines (cytokine storm) → Hyperinflammation

Host factors: Age, Smoking, Diabetes, Glucocorticoids

Failure of adaptive response can lead to hyperinflammation via macrophage activation

Neutralizing antibodies → block viral entry.

Non-neutralizing antibodies → enhance uptake of virus into macrophages via Fc receptors, activating them through TLR signaling and causing hyperinflammation (cytokine storm).

Appearance of neutralizing antibodies

The graph shows that neutralizing antibodies (NAbs) against SARS-CoV-2 begin to appear around day 7–10 after symptoms start and reach strong levels by day 10–15. After that, the titers stay high or plateau.

Lung immunity highlights Type 2 immune responses

Helminths / Allergens → Sensor (Epithelium) → 1st order cytokine (IL-25, IL-33, TSLP) → Lymphocyte (ILC, Th2) → 2nd order cytokine (IL-5, IL-9, IL-13, AREG) → Effector (Goblet cells, Mast cells, Eosinophils) → Response (Allergic inflammation, Helminth expulsion, Tissue repair)

Exposure to allergens, viruses & bacteria makes inflamed epithelial cells release cytokines (alarmins) → Activate dendritic cells (DCs) and innate lymphoid cells (ILC-2)

Dendritic cell activation leads to:

Differentiation of naive T cells into effector T cells

B cell activation → Class switching → IgE production

Smooth muscle contraction

Eosinophil activation

→ Allergic asthma

Innate lymphoid cell activation leads to:

IgE production

Smooth muscle contraction

Eosinophil activation

→ Non-allergic asthma

IMMUNOLOGY OF THE HEART - CARDIO-IMMUNOLOGY

Cardiac immunology

Study of the immune cells & pathways that modulate homeostasis in cardiac function

Regulate the balance between tissue damage & tissue repair

Resident macrophages - Main role in heart homeostasis

Origin: Embryonic → Migrate to the heart before delivery

Most abundant immune cell type in the heart

Identified by CX3CR1 expression

M2-type anti-inflammatory phenotype (in homeostasis state)

Single cell RNA sequencing highlights the complexity of cardiac resident microphages

Divide into sub-populations by:

Origin

Life cycle

Transcriptomic signature

Location in the heart (niche)

LYVE-1+ macrophages & arterial stiffness

LYVE-1+ macrophages coat murine & human blood vessels

LYVE-1+ deficiency → Arterial stiffness & Collagen deposition:

Hyaluronan - LYVE-1+ interaction → metalloprotease-9 (MMP-9) dependent collagen degradation → Maintain arterial homeostasis

No hyaluronan-LYVE-1+ interaction → No collagen degradation → Arterial stiffness & Collagen deposition

Resident macrophages - Main role in heart integrity

Myeloid Epithelial Reproductive Tyrosine Kinase (MERTK) receptor: major macrophage apoptotic cell (AC) receptor

CD36: scavenger receptor recognizing oxidized phospholipids & lipoproteins → Internalization of apoptotic cells

CD36 activates Nr4a1, which induces MERTK expression, enabling macrophages to survive and perform phagocytosis

→ Remove apoptotic cells & control inflammation

Resident macrophages - Clearance of cell debris alleviates inflammatory pathways

Cardiolipin (TLR2 ligand) and mtDNA (TLR9 ligand) are mitochondrial DAMPs released during cell injury.

They activate TLR2/TLR9 → Activate MyD88 pathways → Activate transcription factors: NF-κB (early cytokines) and IRF (type I IFN).

→ Drives strong sterile inflammation, especially in the heart after injury.

Resident macrophages: Electrical conduction via Connexin 43

Depletion of macrophage → Cardiomyocyte depolarization is not passed from atria to ventricles

→ Atrioventricular (AV) block

Classic AV block pattern: Normal P waves but rare or uncoupled QRD complexes

→ Immediate treatment with pacemaker

Connexin 43: gap junction protein that forms electrical channels between cells → Allows rapid ion flow and coordinated heart contraction.

Resident macrophages produces Amphiregulin (Type 2 immune response that phosphorylates Connexin 43

Amphiregulin (AREG):

Produced by cardiac macrophages

Phosphorylate Connexin 43

→ Stabilize gap junctions & enhance electrical coupling

(deletion of AREG from macrophages lead to gap junction disorganization → lethal arrhythmia)

3 roles of cardiac macrophages

Debris removal to keep mitochondrial integrity (MERTK, CD36 - major macrophage apoptotic cell receptor)

Enhance electrical coupling of cardiomyocytes (production of Amphiregulin - AREG → Phosphorylate Connexin 43 → Stabilization)

LYVE-1+ macrophages coat human blood vessels → degrade collagen → maintain arterial homeostasis

Resident macrophages: electrical conduction via Connexin 43 gap junctions with cardiomyocytes

Macrophages make direct contact with cardiomyocytes via Connexin 43 (CX43)-containing gap junctions

Positive charge travels through gap junction (cardiomyocyte - macrophage)

Direction of cation flux:

Diastole (relaxing heart): macrophage → cardiomyocyte

Systole (contracting heart): cardiomyocyte → macrophage

Deprivation of K+ in diet induces arrhythmia storm post myocardial infarction (MI)

K+ efflux is required to return the membrane to its resting potential

Low potassium levels post-MI rapidly increases the neutrophil level and decrease the resident macrophage level

→ Increased inflammation & unsynchronized electrical

signals lead to arrhythmia storm post-MI

Ventricular tachycardia - Neutrophils & Macrophages

Depletion of neutrophils → Reduces ventricular tachycardia (VT)

Depletion of macrophages → Increases VT

Summary - Neutrophils & Macrophages in cardiac conduction

Hypokalemia → Neutrophil recruitment → Lipocalin-2 production → Oxidative stress → Electrical storm

Myocardial infarction → Monocyte / Macrophage recruitment → Clear up cellular debris via CD36 & MERTK receptors → Stabilizes cardiac conduction

Neonate & Adult T cell regeneration capability

Neonate → MI induction → Cardiac regeneration (rapid resolution of immune response)

Adult T cell transfer → MI induction → Impaired cardiac regeneration (sustained inflammatory response)

Immune-mediated remodelling steps

Heart injury triggers innate (neutrophils, macrophages) and then adaptive (T/B cell) immune responses.

Acute inflammation helps clear debris and repair tissue, but persistent immune activation causes chronic inflammation, fibrosis, and pathological cardiac remodeling leading to heart failure.

Hypertrophy - A cardiac adaptation

Physiological hypertrophy:

10 - 20% increase in heart mass

Cardiomyocytes become longer & thicker

Can be seen in athletes & pregnant women

Not permanent

Do NOT progress to heart failure

Pathological hypertrophy

Fibrosis & cell death

Evolves into heart failure

2 types of pathological hypertrophy:

HFpEF (heart failure with preserved ejection fraction): thicker walls, reduced volume but normal ejection

HFrEF (heart failure with reduced ejection fraction): chambers dilate, thinner walls, impaired ejection

Cardiac immune response is dictated by the different types of cardiac injury

Post-MI cardiac remodelling: Replacement fibrosis

Post-TAC (transverse aortic constriction) cardiac remodelling:

Low grade inflammation

Reactive fibrosis

Capillary rarefaction

Post Antigen-induced DCM (dilated cardiomyopathy) cardiac remodelling:

Reactive / Replacement fibrosis

Ventricular dilation

Cardiomyocyte death induces different types of fibrosis - Replacement & Reactive fibrosis

Replacement fibrosis:

Associated with myocardial infarction (MI)

Non-contractile, non-elastic tissue

Reactive fibrosis:

Occurs if there is no large scale cardiomyocyte death

2 histologically different forms:

Interstitial fibrosis:

Fibrosis between cardiomyocytes

Associated with chronic stressors (hypertension, aging,...)

Perivascular fibrosis

Fibrosis around blood vessels

Associated with diabetes & hypertension

Immune-mediated fibrosis formation phases

Inflammatory phase: cardiomyocyte death → recruitment of neutrophils & macrophages

Proliferation / granulation phase: reduced inflammatory response

Maturation phase: formation of fibrotic scares

Balance is crucial:

Inflammation > Proliferation → Cardiac rupture

Inflammation < Proliferation → Fibrosis / HFrEF

Cardiac fibroblasts can act as APCs under TAC (transaortic constriction)

In pressure-overload hypertrophy, cardiac fibroblasts act as antigen-presenting cells that activate Th1 CD4 T cells through MHCII and IFN-γ, driving reactive fibrosis and cardiac dysfunction.

Role of regulatory CD69+ T cells in post-MI model

This paper shows that a special subset of regulatory T cells (Tregs) that express CD69 is critical for survival, limiting infarct size, and preventing harmful inflammation after a heart attack.

Increased CD69+ Treg → lower rehospitalization rate

Potential therapeutic target to prevent HF after MI

→ CD69 expression on Tregs protect patients from immune damage after MI

Immunological modulation of ion channels & gap junctions

Autoantibodies and inflammatory cytokines modify calcium, sodium, potassium channels or gap junctions

→ Disrupt the heart’s electrical signals

→ Lead to bradycardia, tachycardia, AV block, AF, or ventricular fibrillation.

Anti-Ro/SSA antibodies

An antibody found in patients with autoimmune diseases (ADs)

Also found in some healthy subjects

Most investigated form of autoimmune LQTS (long QT syndrome)

Direct inhibition of hERG potassium channel → Delay ventricular repolarization

High levels of anti-Ro/SSA Ab → Increased risk of LQTS (independent of AD history)

→ Contribute to ventricular arrhythmia & cardiac arrest

Lung immunology

Mucosal epithelium type 1 & 2

Type I mucosal surfaces: gut, respiratory, female upper reproductive systems → Simple columnar epithelial layer in which IgA is the major immunoglobulin type

Type II mucosal surfaces: eye, mouth, female lower reproductive systems → Have a stratified squamous epithelial layer, and the main immunoglobulin is IgG

Different cells of the epithelium and their role: goblet cells, ciliated cells, basal cells, alveoli type I & II

Ciliated cells: clearance of mucus

Goblet cells: secretion of mucus

Club (Clara cells): secretion of protective glycosaminoglycans

Basal cells: progenitor cells

Alveoli:

Alveoli type I: facilitate gas exchange

Alveoli type II: secrete pulmonary surfactant

Gut immunology

Different cells that make up the gut epithelium & their roles: goblet cells, Paneth cells, M cells, enterocytes, intraepithelial immune cells (CD8+)

Goblet cells: mucus production

Paneth cells: anti-microbial activity

M cells: role in starting immune response (put the immune cells close enough together)

Enterocytes: the main epithelial cell of the gut

Intraepithelial immune cells (CD8+): preserving infection-free gut epithelial cells

The structure of gut mucosal barrier

Firm inner layer

Loose outer layer

Commensal vs. Pathogenic bacteria immune response

Commensal bacteria:

Production of immuno-suppressive components → Inhibit DC maturation

Immature DC give weak co-stimulatory signals → Induce CD4+ to differentiate into Treg cells

Pathogenic bacteria:

Invasive microorganisms penetrate epithelium & activate DCs

Activated DCs express all 3 signals → T cell activation → pro-inflammatory environment

→ Dysregulated state: dysbiosis

→ Non-specific inflammation, acute inflammation of the gut

IgA:

Main immunoglobulin that control the integrity of the gut flora

2 types of IgA production:

T-cell independent activation of B cells

T-cell dependent activation of B cells

The different types of IgA

Unlike serum IgA, secretory IgA is dimeric

Subclass:

IgA1: more abundant in the intestine & serum, less protease resistant than IgA2

IgA2: more abundant in the colon, more protease resistant than IgA1

IgA will control the gut microbial composition

Two ways of excluding bacteria

Agglutination: dimeric conformation → bind & make pathogens stick together (occur at high concentrations)

Enchained-growth: like a bacterium to its own daughter cells after binary fission (occur at low bacterial densities)

IgA will act in 3 different ways:

Preventing colonization of pathogenic bacteria

Promoting colonization of beneficial bacteria

Influence bacteria behaviour though gene regulation

Examples:

Immunoregulation between Bacteroidetes & Helicobacter hepaticus

Balance between IL-10 (Treg) & IL-17/IL-23 (Th17)

Two types of inflammatory bowel disease (IBD)

Ulcerative colitis (continuous inflammation)

Crohn’s disease (patchy inflammation)

IBD is a dysregulation of the balance between Th17/Treg

Loss of mucus barrier

Pathogenic colonization of bacteria

→ Inflammation

Immunotherapy JAK/STAT that target cytokine pathways

JAK-STAT pathway

JAK-STAT pathway influence immune cell differentiation and development & has immunoregulatory effects

Targeting this pathway has immunoregulatory effects

Liver immunology

Liver:

Physiological link between molecules from the gut & systemic circulation

Portal vein:

Supplies 80% of liver blood flow

Low-pressure system

Carries significant amounts of bacterial products & other foreign systems that are introduced to the immune system

Immunological roles of hepatocytes (in addition to metabolic roles)

Production of plasma protein: clotting factors, complement & anti-microbial proteins

Production of acute phase proteins upon local & systemic infection

Antigen presentation to T cells within the liver

Cellular structure of the liver (hepatocytes, LSEC, immune cells - Kupffer cells (KC), hepatic stellate cells)

Most of the liver is hepatocytes

The liver is also connected to the intestine via bile secretion (bile ducts are appearing on liver tissue slide)

A blood-filtering organism that filters blood from the entire body

Blood enters venal port and and create a meshwork of liver sinusoids

Liver sinusoidal endothelial cells (LSECs):

Specialized endothelial cells located in the liver

Form part of the hepatic sinusoids

High permeability

Role:

Filter blood

Eliminate soluble substances during inflammation

Immune cells in the liver are found in:

The sinusoids

The subendothelial area (the space of Dissé)

Majority of immune cells in the liver are macrophages (called Kupffer cells - KCs)

Kupffer cells (KCs)

Have phagocytic activity

Secrete both pro-inflammatory & anti-inflammatory substances

→ Dual role in mediating tolerogenic defence & inflammatory responses

Hepatic stellate cells play a role in:

Development

Regeneration

Immune functions

Angiogenesis

Storage of vitamin A (Retinol)

Cross-talk Gut-Liver

Gut-Liver connection (slide 59-60)

Normal condition:

Lumen pH, lumen enzyme & bile production (from the liver) → limit pathogen colonization

Anti-microbial peptide (produced by Paneth cells from the intestine) & IgA → prevent pathogen colonization

Mucus (produced by goblet cells) → primary physical barrier

Intestinal epithelium (layer of columnar epithelial cells joined by tight junctions) → secondary physical barrier

Danger condition:

Dendritic cells will induce immune response

DCs travel through afferent lymphatic vessel to reach mesenteric lymph node → Act as a supplementary fence to prevent bacterial colonization

Intestinal blood enters the liver through the portal vein → LSEC & Kupffer cells act as gatekeepers & Limit the entry of microbes into the systemic blood circulation

Two important connections between Gut & Liver

Bile: through the bile duct

Blood: through the portal vein

One connection is used to build the immune response through lymphatic vessel and lymph node

Lymph goes back to blood-systemic circulation via the thoracic duct

Functional interaction between Gut-Liver-Lymph Node

Within the lumen of the gut

Bile factors:

Secretory IgA that will then be transendocytosed through epithelial cells

Primary bile acid that will be transformed into secondary bile acid by gut bacteria

Retinoids (tolerogenic anti-inflammatory) (transformed by DCs into retinoic acid (RA) → Promote Treg differentiation & their homing to the gut

Other bacterial metabolites: converted within the gut lumen from nutrient / host-derived substrates into SCFA, lactate, indole

These metabolites / bacterial-associated motifs will enforce epithelial gut integrity by

Inducing intra-epithelial lymphocyte proliferation

Inducing mucus production

Anti-microbial peptide secretion

Induce tolerogenic potential of dendritic cells & macrophages

Macrophage derived cytokine (IL-10) or 2nd bile acid will help to promote & balance the proportion of Treg (FoxP3) & Th17 (RORgt). Other immune cells maintain epithelial barrier through production of IL-22

T cell populations enter the circulation via afferent lymphatic vessel & can migrate to peripheral organs, including gut & liver

Microbial metabolites & pathogen-associated molecular patterns (PAMPs) reach the liver through the liver portal vein → Help in the positioning of Kupffer cells & control their phenotype (regulatory vs inflammatory)

Cross-talk Gut-Heart

Intestinal bacteria can express molecular patterns that mimic self-antigen pattern

→ Trigger organ-specific autoimmune dysregulation

→ Organ damage

Example:

Ag from Bacteroidetes species appears to be specific for myosin 4 peptide

Th17 enter the systemic circulation → autoimmune response towards cardiac tissue - MOLECULAR MIMICRY

→ Autoimmune diseases can be triggered by:

Individual genetic profile

Environmental factors (diet, pollution,...)

Infectious disease history

CARDIAC ENERGY METABOLISM

Key concepts

Structure & Contraction

Function

Mitochondrial genes & electron transport chain

Biogenesis

Fission & Fusion

Heart failure & Bioenergetic modulations in HF

Mitochondrial biogenesis in HF

Dysregulation of master regulator

Cardiomyocyte

Cardiac muscle cell

30% of cardiac cell

Morphology:

Branches

Diameter 10 - 15 µm

Length 100 µm

Links: intercalated disks

Cellular architecture & contraction

Highly organized intracellular architecture

Myofilaments: 50% of the cell volume

Mitochondria: 30 - 40% of the cell volume

Free cytosol: < 5% of the cell volume

Energy consumption

Main energy consumers in the cardiomyocyte:

Myosin ATPase

SERCA

Membrane transporters

Heart:

High energy demand (1 mM ATP/s)

Low energy stock (8mM ATP and 15 mM PCr)

→ Requires constant energy production

Energy substrates of the myocardium

Many kinds of substrates

Normal conditions: fatty acids

Mitochondria produce 90 - 95% of energy

Mitochondria

Main function in cardiac energetics

Components

Outer membrane

Intermembrane space

Inner membrane with cristae

Matrix

Mitochondrion:

“Mitos”: filament → After fusion

“Chondros”: seed → After fission

→ Different observations after fusion & fission

Mitochondria in adult cardiomyocytes

5000 - 10 000 mitochondria per cell

Mean volume 1µm^3

Mitochondria occupy 30 - 40% of the cell volume

Mitochondria & cardiomyocyte cytoarchitecture

Precise arrangement of components within cytosol

Highly specialized cytoskeleton:

Microtubule

Actin

Intermediate filaments (desmin)

Emergence of microdomains:

Between mitochondria & myofilament

Between mitochondria and SR

Cellular Respiration

Glycolysis

Takes place in the cytoplasm

Anaerobic process

Products of glycolysis:

2 Pyruvate

2 NADH

2ATP

Intermediate step

Takes place in the mitochondrial matrix

Pyruvate → Acetyl-CoA → Krebs cycle

Krebs cycle

Takes place in the mitochondrial matrix

Series of chemical reactions from Acetyl-CoA

Release of reducing agents (NADH, FADH2)

Output of the cycle:

1 ATP

2 NADH

1FADH2

Electron transport chain (ETC) & Oxidative phosphorylation

Electron transfer in the chain

→ Proton translocation to mitochondrial intermembrane space

→ H+ accumulation in the intermembrane space

→ Electrochemical gradient

→ Passage of H+ to ATP synthase

→ ADP → ATP (26-34 ATP)

Net ATP yield after aerobic respiration: 30 - 38 ATP

ATP synthase (= nanomotor / turbine)

Protons flow through a channel in the ATP synthase

This movement spins the protein

→ Generates energy to add an inorganic phosphate group to ADP → Create ATP

Regulation of energy production

Increase in cytosolic calcium

→ Increase calcium in mitochondrial matrix

→ Stimulate: mt dehydrogenase & ATP synthase

→ Higher ATP & NADH production

Increase in cytosolic ADP

→ Stimulate ATP synthase

→ Higher ATP production

Energy transfer systems

Required because of high myofilament & mitochondria density

→ Low efficiency of energy diffusion

Two energy transfer systems:

Creatine Kinase shuttle

Long-distance ATP delivery

Mechanism:

At the mitochondria: ATP + Kinase is converted by Creatine Kinase into PCr (phosphocreatine) + ADP

At the myofibrils / SR: PCr + ADP is used by Creatine Kinase to create ATP + Creatine

→ Energy shuttle for ATP delivery

Direct adenine nucleotide channeling (DANC)

Rapid local ATP transfer

Mechanism:

Occurs when mitochondria is located near ATP-consuming structures

ATP moves from mitochondria directly to ATP-consuming structures (myofilaments / SR)

Mitochondrial genes

Mitochondrial DNA

Circular

About 16 500 nucleotide pairs

13 proteins (related to the ETC) → 5 complexes (I - V)

22 tRNA

2 rRNA

Nuclear DNA

1000 mitochondrial proteins

Precursor

Transcription regulation

PGC-1α:

Peroxisome proliferator–activated receptor gamma coactivator-1 alpha

Master regulator of mitochondrial biogenesis & cardiac energy metabolism

Is a transcription coactivator → Regulate transcription

Plays a role in

Mitochondrial biogenesis

Expression of mitochondrial proteins

Glucose utilization

Angiogenesis

Phospholipid biosynthesis

FA oxidation

Β-oxidation

Antioxidants

Detoxification systems

Mitochondrial life cycle

Mitochondrial biogenesis

Proliferation of mitochondria through division

Process:

mtDNA replication

Transcription of nuclear DNA & mt DNA

Import precursors in cytosol to mt compartments

Complex assembly

Lipid & phospholipid synthesis

Mitochondrial dynamics

Fission:

Division of a mitochondrion into several daughter mitochondria

Main regulators:

DRP1 (Dynamin-related protein 1): wrap around & split mitochondrion into daughter mitochondria

Fis1 (Fission protein 1): helps recruit & anchor DRP1 to mitochondrial surface

Fusion:

Physical merging of outer & inner membranes of 2 separate mitochondria.

Main regulators:

Mitofusins (Mfn1 / Mfn2): fusion of outer membrane

OPA1 (Optic atrophy 1): fusion of inner membrane

Mitochondrial networks:

Is divided into 2 types:

Fused mitochondrial network: efficient & protective

Fragmented mitochondrial network: stress response, division & apoptosis

According to:

Cell type (cardiomyocytes, immune cells, …)

Cellular environment ( energetics, ROS, …)

Cellular life cycle

Regulated by:

Profusion & profission protein expression

Dynamin activity modulation (PTMs)

Consequences on

Cell death resistance

Bioenergetic efficiency

OPA1 deficiency → Promote mitochondrial network fragmentation → Proof of mt dynamics

Mitophagy

Selective autophagy of mitochondria

Involvement of specific machinery: PINK1 - Parkin

Normal mitochondria: PINK1 is imported & degraded

Damaged mitochondria:

PINK1 accumulates on the mt outer membrane

PINK1 recruits Parkin

PINK1-Parkin complex recruits ubiquitin

Ubiquitin binds P62

P62 recognizes LC3B on autophagosome membrane

Autophagosome engulfs the mitochondria

Fusion of autophagosome & lysosome

→ Damaged mt are digested

Mitophagy in the heart

Mitophagy is required to fully recover after sepsis

Heart failure

Inability of the heart to provide adequate blood flow to meet the needs of the organism

Is associated with energy metabolism alterations

Assessing mitochondrial oxidative capacity

Mitochondria in respiratory solution

Measure oxygen level

Add different substrates to feed the mitochondria

Add ADP to assess the ETC efficiency

Bioenergetic modulations in heart failure

Dramatically reduce mitochondrial ATP production

Decreased level of citrate synthase

Decrease oxidative capacities

Disrupt the creatine kinase energy transfer system

Leading to an energy-starved, failing heart.

Weaken the mt biogenesis process

Decreased level of PGC-1α, NRF-2 & TFAM

(key transcriptional regulators of mt biogenesis)

Dysregulation of PGC-1α - the master regulator

→ Decreased mt biogenesis, mt protein synthesis, glucose utilization, angiogenesis, phospholipid biosynthesis, FA oxidation, detoxification systems, …

cAMP-PDEs in SA node

Additional regulators of cardiac automaticity

Genesis of membrane potential

Lipid bilayer permeability

Only allow passive diffusion of gas & small polar uncharged molecules

Types of transport:

Facilitated diffusion: no ATP, requires protein channels

Passive transport: no ATP, simple diffusion

Primary active transport: transport against the gradient, requires ATP

Secondary active transport: transport against the gradient, uses energy from ion gradients created by primary active transport. Include 2 types:

Symporters: transported molecules move in the same direction

Antiporters: transported molecules move in the opposite direction

Fick’s law of diffusion

For an uncharged molecule, diffusion occurs from the most concentrated to the least concentrated compartment

Reversal potential

The membrane potential at which a specific ion has no net movement across the membrane.

For K⁺, it’s around –90 mV.

Importance: Ion flows in the direction that will push the membrane potential toward its own reversal potential.

Example: When K⁺ channels open → MP goes toward –90 mV → repolarization

Na/K ATPase pump

3 Na+ out

2 K+ in

Uses ATP

→ Maintain electrochemical gradient

→ Maintain electrical activity of excitable cells

Membrane potential

Potential difference between intracellular and extracellular compartment

Calculated by Goldman-Hodgkin-Katz voltage equation (for monovalent ions)

Driving force:

Vm: Membrane potential

Eion: Equilibrium potential = reversal potential

Vm - Eion > 0 mV → cations exit, anions enter → repolarization

Vm - Eion = 0mV → no modification of membrane potential

Vm - Eion < 0 mV → cations enter, anions exit → depolarization

Membrane potential recording

Membrane potential is recorded by inserting a microelectrode into the cell and comparing its voltage to a reference electrode in the extracellular fluid.

The amplifier displays the voltage difference as the membrane potential.

Cardiac electrophysiology

Blood flow through the heart

Body → Right atrium → Right ventricle → Pulmonary artery → Lungs → Pulmonary vein → Left atrium → Left ventricle → Aorta → Entire body

Cardiac conduction system

Sinoatrial node (SA) node: primary pacemaker

Atrioventricular node (AV) node

SA node → AV node → Bundle of His → Purkinje fibers

Electrocardiogram (ECG)

Cardiac action potentials

SA & AV node: slow Ca²⁺-dependent APs (pacemaker type).

Atria, ventricles, Purkinje fibers: fast Na⁺-dependent APs with plateau.

Ventricular action potential

Ventricular cell properties

Stick shape with ramifications (branches to other cells)

Striated (similar to sarcomeres)

Width ~ 25 m, length ~ 100 m, thickness < 20 m

Excitable, contractile, conductive

Excitation-contraction coupling

Calcium enters the cardiomyocyte via L-type calcium channels on the sarcolemma (inside T-tubules)

The calcium binds to ryanodine receptor (RyR) and triggers calcium-induced calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR).

Calcium binds to troponin, which makes tropomyosin slide off actin filaments → myosin heads can bind to actin filaments and pull, which leads to contraction

SERCA pumps calcium back into the SR (uses ATP) → main relaxation mechanism of cardiomyocyte

Sodium-calcium exchanger (NCX): 1 Ca2+ out; 3 Na+ in → important in late relaxation of cardiomyocyte

Excitation-contraction coupling

Patch-Clamp

Electrically isolate a fragment of cellular membrane

Apply current / potential & record response

A current → Current clamp

A potential → Voltage clamp

1991 Nobel Prize in Medicine: Erwin Neher & Bert Sakmann

→ Understand how voltage-gated channels work

→ Study drug mechanisms & understand diseases

Ventricular action potential

Ventricular Action Potential

Membrane resting potential - IK1

Inward rectifier K+ channel

Maintain membrane resting potential at -80 mV

Always open

Heterotetramer: Kir2.1, Kir2.2

Voltage-gated sodium channels - INa

Open at -70 / -60 mV (initial depolarization from NCX)

Pore forming subunit α:

4 domains, each domain has 6 transmembrane proteins

S4: voltage sensor

Subunit α is mainly Nav1.5 in ventricle cardiomyocytes

Blocked with high dose of tetrodotoxin

3 states: close, open, inactivated

Voltage-gated potassium channels

Pore forming subunit α:

4 domains, each domain has 6 transmembrane proteins

S4: voltage sensor

Main subunits: Kvx.x

High level of diversity (40 different channels, 12 distinct groups based on aa sequence homology - Kv1 - Kv12)

Involved in cell repolarization

Voltage-gated potassium channels - Ito

Ito: transient outward potassium current

Involved in early phase of repolarization

In human: homotetramer of Kv4.3 & 1 regulatory subunit KChIP

3 states: close, open, inactivated

Fast activation

Voltage-gated potassium channels - IKs

IKs: slow / delayed potassium current

Slow activation

Blocked by indapamine

Main subunit Kv7.1 (KvLQT1), encoded by KCNQ1 gene, associated with KCNE1 regulatory subunit

Voltage-gated potassium channels - IKr

IKr: is a channel which activates rapidly and for more negative potential than IKs

Involved in early repolarization phase

Blocked by a number of drugs

→ Death risk caused by long QT syndrome-induced torsades de pointes

Voltage-gated calcium channels: ICaL

α subunit is mainly Cav1.2 for L-type calcium channels

Responsible for main entry of Ca2+ in the cardiomyocyte

Key player of excitation contraction coupling

3 states: close, open, inactivated

Blocked by dihydropyridine & verapamil

Sodium-calcium exchanger: NCX

3 Na+ in / 1 Ca2+ out

Electrogenic: induces depolarization

Summary

Phase 0: INa ↑ (Na⁺ in)

Phase 1: Ito ↑ (K⁺ out)

Phase 2: ICaL (in) ≈ IKr/IKs (out)

Phase 3: IKr & IKs ↑↑ (K⁺ out)

Phase 4: IK₁ ↑ (K⁺ stabilizes rest)

Gender differences in ventricular repolarization

Estrogen causes down-regulation of potassium voltage-gated channels → Longer action potential duration (longer QT)

Consequences of gender difference:

Female → longer AP duration → longer QT interval

→ Long QT-induced torsades de pointes

Male → shorter AP duration → shorter QT interval

→ Brugada syndrome, early repolarization, idiopathic VF, short QT syndrome

Circadian ventricular electrical activity

Ventricular repolarization is regulated by the circadian rhythm

→ QT interval is longest at night & shortest during the day

K+ channels are responsible for the circadian regulation of ventricular electrical activity

Electrical coupling of cardiomyocytes - Gap junctions

Gap junctions:

Large conductive pores for ions & small molecules

6 connexin subunits = 1 connexon

2 connexons = 1 gap junction

→ Electrical coupling of myocytes

Electrical coupling of cardiomyocytes - Colocalization of CX43 and Nav1.5 in the perinexus

Perinexus: the small area adjacent to gap junctions (< 30 nm)

Colocalization of connexin and sodium channels

→ Enhance intercellular electrical conductance (electrical coupling of cardiomyocytes)

β-adrenergic stimulation and excitation-contraction coupling

Epinephrine / Norepinephrine bind to β-adrenergic receptor

Activates Gs protein

Stimulates adenylate cyclase (AC)

Increase cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP) production

cAMP activates protein kinase A (PKA)

PKA phosphorylates L-type calcium channel → Increase Ca2+ influx → Stronger contraction → Positive inotropic effect

PKA phosphorylates phospholamban (PLB) → Enhance Ca2+ recapture by SERCA → Faster relaxation → Positive lusitropic effect

Electrophysiological remodelling during cardiac hypertrophy

Post MI, cardiomyocytes become hypertrophic.

This is because of:

Increased L-type calcium current activity

Decreased potassium current (Ito) activity

→ Prolonged AP duration and early afterdepolarizations (EADs).

→ This electrical remodeling greatly increases risk of ventricular arrhythmias.

Electrophysiological remodelling during heart failure

During heart failure:

Inward currents (ICa-L, INa, NCX) increase

Repolarizing K⁺ currents (Ito, IKr, IKs, IK1) decrease

Nav1.5 expression decreases, slowing conduction

→ The result is prolonged APD, Ca²⁺ overload, EADs/DADs, and high arrhythmia risk

Lateralization of CX43 abdominal aortic constriction

During pressure-overload hypertrophy, Cx43 redistributes from intercalated discs to the lateral membranes (‘lateralization’).

→ This disrupts gap-junction organization, slows conduction, increases anisotropy, and predisposes the heart to reentrant arrhythmias.

Sinoatrial node (SA node)

Pacemaker of the heart

Cell types in rabbit SA node:

Types of cell in rabbit SA node:

Elongated spindle cell

Spindle cell

Spider cell

Atrial cell

Contain cell types with different morphology & action potentials:

In the central area: good automaticity, not so good conductivity

Peripheral area: good conductivity to spread the signal

Two conceptual models for sinoatrial transition

Gradient model: gradual transition from typical nodal cells to atrial cells

Mosaic model: mosaic transition from typical nodal cells to atrial cells

Genesis of cardiac automaticity

Pacemaker potential: slow, spontaneous depolarization during phase 4 (do not have a stable resting membrane potential like other cardiomyocytes)

This pacemaker potential is generated by:

Funny current If

Calcium channels (T-type & L-type)

IK decay → Less K+ go out → Slowly depolarizes

NCX

Funny current - If

It behaves unusually because it is activated during hyperpolarization

Is a mixed cation current (Na+ in, K+ out)

→ Slow depolarization towards threshold potential

HCN channels generate If

HCN channel: Hyperpolarization-activated Cyclic Nucleotide–gated channel (in SA node: HCN 1 & HCN 4)

Ivabradine

Binds inside HCN channel pore → Reduces If

→ Decreases the pacemaker potential slope

→ Pure HR reduction (reduce slow diastolic depolarization without inotropic effect)

Brand name: Procoralan

Autonomic regulation of cardiac automaticity

Parasympathetic regulation (Vagus nerve → ACh)

Neurotransmitter: ACh

Receptor: M2 muscarinic receptor

→ Slow down heart rate by decreasing phase 4 depolarization slope

Sympathetic regulation (Cardiac nerves → NE)

Neurotransmitter: Norepinephrine

Receptor: β₁-adrenergic receptor

→ Speed up heart rate by increasing phase 4 depolarization slope

Sympathetic regulation of pacemaker activity

NE β₁-adrenergic receptor → Activate Gs protein → Activate Adenylate Cyclase (AC) → Increase cAMP level

Voltage clock (membrane potential):

cAMP binds HCN channel → Increases If

PKA phosphorylates L-type calcium channel → Increases I Ca,L

Increased calcium channel → Increases INCX

Calcium clock (calcium release & reuptake from SR):

PKA phosphorylates RyR → Increase Ca2+ release from SR

PKA phosphorylate PLB → Increase CA2+ reuptake by SERCA into SR

→ Increase heart rate → Positive chronotropic effect

→ Increase contractility → Positive inotropic effect

→ Increase relaxation → Positive lusitropic effect

Parasympathetic regulation of pacemaker activity

Acetylcholine (ACh) + M2 muscarinic receptor (M2R) → Activate Gi protein → Inhibit cAMP production

Decrease HCN function → Decrease If

Decrease L-type calcium channel activity → Decrease CICR

Decrease PLB activity → Decrease SERCA Calcium reuptake

→ Slow down both the voltage clock & the calcium clock

→ Negative chronotropic, inotropic & lusitropic effect

Effects of autonomic agonists on If current

Isoproterenol shifts the If activation curve to more positive voltages and increases If, accelerating pacemaker rate

ACh shifts it to more negative voltages and reduces If, slowing pacemaker rate

Phosphodiesterases (PDEs) and pacemaker activity

Phosphodiesterases degrade cAMP (cyclic adenosine monophosphate), reducing If, ICa,L, and Ca²⁺ cycling

→ Slowing pacemaker depolarization and opposing sympathetic increases in heart rate

Cardiac cAMP-Phosphodiesterases (PDEs)

Cardiac PDEs (PDE1, 2, 3, 4, 8) degrade cAMP

→ Control β-adrenergic signaling, pacemaker activity, and Ca²⁺ clock

cAMP-PDEs in SA node

Blocking PDEs in the SA node increases cAMP, which accelerates diastolic depolarization via If and Ca²⁺ currents, causing a significant increase in heart rate

IBMX: non-specific PDE inhibitor

PDE3: main regulator of cAMP & heart rate in SA node

The best effect in increasing heart rate is seen when PDE3 & PDE4 is inhibited at the same time

In vivo, PDE overexpression leads to decreased heart rate & worse survival curve

Additional regulators of cardiac automaticity

Histamine, serotonin, neurotensin, angiotensin, …

EXCITATION-CONTRACTION COUPLING (ECC)

Key concepts

Excitation contraction coupling

Cardiomyocyte structure

Myofilament

Cardiac excitation contraction coupling

Species differences in ventricular cardiomyocyte Ca2+ fluxes

Method of study of ECC

Confocal microscopy

Patch-clamp coupled to confocal microscopy

Key regulators of the cardiac ECC

Cellular structure

Transverse tubules (TT)

Dyadic cleft

Ca2+-induced Ca2+ release (CICR)

NCX

FKBP12.6 (Calstabin)

SERCA

L-type calcium channel

Beta-adrenergic regulation of ECC by EPAC

Excitation contraction coupling

The conversion of an electrical signal to mechanical force through Ca2+ signalling

Cardiomyocyte properties

Long, striated, mono or polynuclear

Excitable, contractile, conductive

Myofilament & cardiomyocyte structure

Actin, myosin

Ca2+ binds to troponin → moves tropomyosin → expose actin → bind to myosin head

Pulling motion towards M-line

Z-line: connection site between sarcolemma

T-tubules (transverse tubules): has many L-type Ca2+ channels → Ca2+ rapid uptake

Cardiac excitation contraction coupling

Depolarization: Sarcolema

Ca2+ influx

T-tubules

L-type calcium channel

Calcium-induced calcium release

Sarcoplasmic reticulum (SR)

RyR

Contraction:

Myofilaments

Relaxation:

SERCA

NCX

Species difference in ventricular cardiomyocyte Ca2+ influx

In rodent, SERCA pump is the main contributor to relaxation compared to rabbit

Method of study of ECC

Confocal microscopy

Diastole → no Ca2+ release

Systole → Ca2+ release + cardiomyocyte contraction

Patch-clamp coupled with confocal microscopy

Patch-clamp + confocal imaging allows simultaneous measurement of Ca²⁺ current (ICaL), intracellular Ca²⁺ release, and contraction

→ Directly linking membrane depolarization to excitation–contraction coupling in a single cardiac cell

Dyadic cleft

Dyad = 1 T-tubule + 1 SR

→ Connected to a network of SR

→ Rapid Ca2+ transfer

RyR regulation

Ca2+:

More Ca2+ → Open

Less Ca2+ → Close

FKBP12.6 (Calstabin): stabilization of RyR closed state during diastole

Less FKBP12.6 than normal → Ca2+ leakiness

More FKBP12.6 than normal → Ca2+ is not released

Overexpression of FKBP12.6 stabilizes RyR2, prevents diastolic Ca²⁺ leak, increases SR Ca²⁺ load, produces larger Ca²⁺ transients, and enhances contraction.

In short → more Ca²⁺ stored + more Ca²⁺ released = stronger, healthier contraction.

Calmodulin (CaM):

Direct RyR binding → Inhibition of RyRs

→ Stabilization of RyRs in a close state

Phosphorylation of RyRs:

PKA (S2808, S2030) and CaMKII (S2814) phosphorylate RyR2, increasing channel activity.

In excess, this causes FKBP12.6 dissociation, SR Ca²⁺ leak, arrhythmias, and heart failure progression.

Luminal Ca2+:

Low → RyR closes

High → RyR opens

Mechanism: Calsequestrin/Triadin/Junctin (CSQ/Tr/Jn) complex

CSQ: sensor, inhibits RyR opening for low Ca2+ concentrations

Tr/Jn: Anchor CSQ to RyR

Low Ca2+ concentration → CSQ attach to Tr/Jn → RyR closes

High Ca2+ concentration → CSQ detaches from Tr/Jn → RyR opens

Beta-adrenergic regulation of ECC by EPAC

EPAC is a cAMP-activated, PKA-independent pathway in β-adrenergic signaling.

It activates CaMKII, increases SR Ca²⁺ load, sensitizes RyR2, and promotes Ca²⁺ leak.

EPAC contributes to arrhythmias and pathological remodeling rather than normal inotropic responses.

RENAL PATHOPHYSIOLOGY

Renal Physiology For Beginners

Fundamental processes of nephron function

Filtration

120 mL / min = 180 L / d

Reabsorption

~179 L / d

Secretion

Definite urine: 1L / d

Renal clearance

Definition: Volume of plasma COMPLETELY cleared of substance by the kidney per unit time

Less than 120 mL/min → Filtered & Completely absorbed

Equal to 120 mL/min → Filtered (neither absorbed nor secreted)

More than 120 mL/min → Filtered & Completely secreted

Renal function estimation

Creatinine clearance: CL = 125 - 130 mL/min

If Creatine clearance > 90 mL/min → Normal renal function

Passive mechanisms of filtration

Hydrostatic pressure: pressure from the blood pressure

Oncotic pressure: pressure from the water pulled back into the bloodstream by proteins

Filtration pressure: a combination of the 2 pressures: 15 mmHg

Renal tubule functions

Reabsorption (especially Na⁺/K⁺ ATPase everywhere)

Active ion pumping (H⁺, K⁺)

Transport of glucose, amino acids, electrolytes

Secretion of drugs/toxins

Passive reabsorption of water (due to osmolality = cortico-medullar concentration gradient)

→ Passive + Active phenomena

Renal pathologies

Alterations of glomerular filtration

Glomerular lesion (glomerulonephritis)

Passing of proteins through the filter

Strong decrease in oncotic pressure

Strong increase in glomerular filtration rate (GFR)

Leads to:

Proteinuria: protein (normally albumin) in urine

Hematuria: blood in urine

→ Damaged glomerular filtration

Nephrotic syndrome

Glomerulonephritis is caused by infectious or autoimmune causes → albuminuria / hematuria → Decrease in oncotic pressure

Water leaks from blood vessel due to osmolality → Increase interstitial volume → Edema (keeping the cup)

Due to low blood volume, Aldosterone level increases, and the patient feels thirsty, making the situation even worse

Therapies of nephrotic syndrome

Lifestyle modifications: low sodium, low protein diet, fluid restriction

Follow up: BP, renal function, proteinuria

Medications:

Corticosteroids: first-line treatment

Immunosuppressants: for patients that do not respond to corticosteroids

Angiotensin-Converting Enzyme (ACE) inhibitors / Angiotensin II receptor blockers → reduce proteinuria & blood pressure

Diuretics: lower BP & reduce edema

New potential therapies:

Monoclonal Abs: treat autoimmune causes of glomerulonephritis

HDAC inhibitors: modify gene expression → protect podocytes from injury

Drug repurposing

Interstitial nephritis

Global renal inflammation: inflammation of the kidney interstitium surrounding the tubules, decreasing tubule function

Causes: bacterial infection, mineral deposits, toxins, allergens

Bacterial infection → Fever

Early tubular lesions (LH, CD):

Decreased sodium reabsorption, decreased urine concentration, decreased H+ secretion

Polyuria & nocturia: pee a lot & pee at night

Natriuresis: pee out salt because of damaged sodium reabsorption,

Alkalinic urine (fail to secrete H+)

Late tubular lesions (Glomerule, PCT, DCT):

Decreased glucose, amino acid & small protein reabsorption

Glycosuria: glucose in urine

Proteinuria: protein in urine

Leucocyturia: WBCs in urine (if infection)

→ Evolution towards KF

Acute kidney failure (AKF) = Acute renal failure (ARF)

Sudden interruption of renal function

Pre-renal causes: renal hypo-perfusion (not enough blood flows to the kidney) = Functional AKF

Hypotension, hypovolemia (low blood volume): due to shock, hemorrhage, heart failure

Dehydration

Diuretics intoxication

→ Symptoms:

Decreased glomerular filtration

Maintained tubular functions

Oliguria (peeing very little)

Renal causes: Renal dysfunction = Organic AKF

Glomerular lesions, tubular lesions, ischemia

Symptoms:

Alteration of glomerular functions

Alteration of tubular functions

Oliguria / Polyuria: peeing too little or too much

Post-renal causes: urine elimination blockage - Obstructive AKF

Obstructions: lithiasis (kidney stone), prostate hypertrophy, tumors

Symptoms:

No alteration of glomerular function

Maintained tubular function

Oliguria, anuria: very little or no pee

Chronic kidney failure - Chronic kidney disease (CKD)

Progressive decline in kidney functions

Often irreversible

Progressive nephron damage & disappearance of nephron over time due to chronic diseases:

Diabetes

Hypertension

Other renal pathologies (glomerulonephritis, polycystosis)

Symptoms:

Creatinemia & Uremia → Decreased filtration function

Hyper-filtration, proteinuria, polyuria, dehydration → Compensated KD

CKD risk factors

Hypertension

Diabetes

Obesity

Cardiovascular disease

Family history

Previous AKF

Creatinine clearance

Less than 90 mL / min

Categories:

Minor CKD: 90 - 60 mL / min → No signs

Moderate CKD: 60 - 30 mL / min → Moderate signs

Advanced CKD: 30 - 15 mL / min → Clinical signs

Terminal CKD: < 15 mL / min → Major complications

CKD consequences

Bone demineralization

Cardiac disease

Hypertension

Edema

GI disorders

Itching

Therapies of CKD

Dietary changes: low sodium, low protein diet, control of fluid intake

Lifestyle: stop smoking, more physical activity, weight control

Medications:

ACEI or ARBs → Control BP

Diuretics → Improve edema & decrease water retention

Statins: manage high cholesterol levels

Phosphate binders: reduce phosphoremia (phosphate in blood → bone density loss)

Advanced treatments:

Dialysis: blood is filtered outside of the body (in the dialysis machine) then returned to the body → Time consuming, expensive, lower quality of life

Kidney transplant

New therapies and approaches:

Autologous cell therapy → own patient’s cell to replace damaged tissue

Xenotransplantation → pig kidney transplantation

GASTROINTESTINAL PATHOPHYSIOLOGY

Pathophysiology of gastric disorders

Nausea & vomiting

Defense reflex mechanism

Pathophysiology

Area postrema (medulla oblongata) - outside BBB

→ Danger alert

→ Activated by emetic substances (dopamine agonists,

drugs, toxins)

Vomiting center (medulla oblongata) - behind BBB

→ Triggers & coordinate vomiting

Triggers of vomiting

Outside the body: toxins, drugs, hypoxia

→ Area postrema → Vomiting center

Inside the body: balance center, brain injury, pregnancy, stomach & digestive tract inflammation

→ Go directly to vomiting center

Mechanism of vomiting

Warning signs (sweat, salivation, nausea, …)

Vomiting:

Anti-peristaltic movement: the stomach squeezes upward

Glottis closure: protect the airway

Powerful abdominal contraction

Cardia opens, esophagus relaxes

Expel gastric content

Complications of vomiting

Malnutrition

Ion losses

Dehydration

Therapies of vomiting

Classical treatments:

Antihistamine: prevent motion sickness

Corticosteroid: reduce inflammation in chemotherapy

Dopamine D2 antagonists: effective in drug-induced nausea (not for children)

New potential therapies:

Combination therapies

Cannabinoids

Gastric electrical stimulation

Ginger spice

Gastritis

Acute / chronic inflammation of the gastric mucosa

90% due to bacteria

Different types of gastritis

NSAIDs, alcohol, lesions → Acute ulcer

H. pylori infection → Chronic ulcer → Cancerous transformation

Autoantibodies → Cancerous transformation → Pernicious anemia (no B12 absorption → low RBC production)

Peptic ulcer

Significant lesion of the wall of the gastric / duodenal mucosa

Pain:

Epigastric / supra-umbilical, torsion-like

Moderate or intense

Relieved by food

90 000 new cases / year (in France)

Pathophysiology:

Imbalance between HCl / Pepsin & protective systems (mucus, bicarbonates)

Alteration of healing capacities of wall

Causes: H. pylori, NSAIDs, IBD, …

Complications: pain, hemorrhages, perforations, …

Therapies of peptic ulcers

Prevention: stop smoking, stop NSAIDs, lower stress levels

Treatments:

Antibiotics → Treat H. pylori infection

Proton Pump Inhibitors (PPIs) → limit gastric acid production

Anti-acids → neutralize stomach acids

H2 blockers → limit acid production

New potential therapies:

New PPIs

Tissue engineering & regenerative medicine → promote wound healing through tissue regeneration

CX43 modulation → speed up healing

Pathophysiology of bowel disorders

Intestinal transit disorders

Stool structures: hard → liquid

→ Graded on a Bristol scale (1: hardest → 7: liquidiest)

→ Normal: Type 3, 4 on bristol scale

Diarrhea

Loose / liquid stools, evacuation > 3 times / day

Etiologies:

Osmotic diarrhea (sorbitol)

Infectious diarrhea (viral infections, bacterial toxins)

Dysenteric bacteria: mucus & blood (bacteria / parasites)

Drug-induced diarrhea (antibiotics, chemotherapies)

IBD (UC, CD)

Treatments:

Hygiene & diet

Intestinal protectors, transit flowers & antisecretory agents, probiotics

Treatment of the cause

Constipation

Hard stools, evacuation less than 3 times / week

Etiologies: Slow down of transit due to

Neurogenic origin (Parkinson, nerve damage)

Medication (opioids, antidepressants)

Functional origin (pregnancy, bed rest)

Evacuation difficulties

Treatments

Hygiene & diet

Laxatives

Functional colopathy - Irritable Bowel Syndrome (IBS)

Alteration of diarrhea & constipation, gas emission, irritation

Chronic inflammatory bowel diseases - Inflammatory bowel diseases (IBD)

Crohn’s disease (CD)

Abscess, fistula, diarrhea, abdominal pain

→ Risk of carcinoma

Hemorrhagic rectocolitis = Ulcerative colitis (UC)

Inflammatory flare ups, remissions, relapses

Treatments:

Corticosteroids → control inflammation (not suitable for long-term usage)

Immunomodulators → suppress immune response

Antibiotics → manage infection complications

New biotherapies:

Anti-TNF agents → prevent inflammation

JAK inhibitors: involved in several cytokine pathways

Pathophysiology of liver disorder

Gallstones (cholelithiasis)

Formation of crystals & stones in the gallbladder

Complications:

Gallbladder inflammation

Painful spasms

Ulceration → Perforation

Definition

Progressive destruction of liver tissue

Inflammation

Replacement with fibrosis

Formation of abnormal hepatocytic nodules (instead of hepatic lobules)

Causes:

Alcohol, iron, lipids

Infectious: Hepatitis B & C

Autoimmune diseases

Metabolism: storage of glucose, protein synthesis, cholesterol (synthesis → elimination), store & process lipo-soluble vitamins (ADEK)

Waste treatment: endogenous waste

Ammonia (NH4+) → Urea

Cholesterol → Bile acids

Bilirubin → glucuro-conjugated bilirubin

Bile production: bile salts, bilirubin, cholesterol

Viral hepatitis B & C

Bleeding

CARDIAC EXCITATION-TRANSCRIPTION MODELLING

Cardiomyocyte processes regulated by Ca2+

Electrical activity

Contraction

Transcription remodelling

Cardiac nuclear structure

2 membranes:

Outer nuclear membrane (ONM)

Inner nuclear membrane (INM)

Interconnected with:

Endoplasmic reticulum

Sarcoplasmic reticulum

Nuclear pore complex (NPC)

Large protein complex

Ca2+ free diffusion

Nuclear sources of Ca2+

Nuclear Ca2+ transient:

Ca2+ diffuses into the nucleus via NPCs

→ Slow but regular

Receptor-mediated nuclear Ca2+ handling

Hormones bind to receptors

Triggers PLC to cleave PIP2 → IP3

IP3 binds to IP3R

Trigger the release of Ca2+ from nearby SRs into the nucleus

→ Direct signalling for transcription regulation

Study of nuclear Ca2+ signalling

Confocal microscopy

Isolate single cells

Use fluorescence to track the Ca2+ signals from the nucleus and the cytoplasm

Differentiate cytoplasm & nucleus Ca2+ signals

→ Understand signalling pathways

→ Comparing different pathologies and healthy cells

Nuclear Ca2+ signals:

Lower, delayed upstroke

Lower peak

Prolonged return to baseline

Nuclear invagination

Deep crevices on the nuclear membrane

→ Faster Ca2+ transfer to the nucleus

Higher invagination density → More rapid, stronger upstroke and more rapid decline of Ca2+ flux

→ Explain pathological difference after heart failure as the nuclear membrane might be altered

Beating frequency influence on nuclear Ca2+ concentration

Nuclear Ca2+: mainly diffusion → slower decay time → nuclear Ca2+ build up when diastole is shortened

Calcineurin/NFAT pathway

During sarcomeric dysfunction, Ca2+ binds to calmodulin

This Ca2+/CaM complex activates Calcineurin

Calcineurin dephosphorylates Nuclear Factor of Activated T cells (NFAT)

Dephosphorylated NFAT travels to the nucleus

NFAT and GATA-4 has a combined effort in producing hypertrophic gene expression

→ This pathway is observed in pathogenic remodelling of cardiomyocytes

Inhibition: can be inhibited by immunomodulators that inhibit Calcineurin such as cyclosporin A (CsA) and FK506

EPAC regulation of Ca2+ Calcineurin/NFAT transcription pathway

Raise of cytosolic Ca2+ release

Activation of NFAT calcineurin dependent cardiomyocyte hypertrophy

Neurohormonal regulation of ETC

The CaMKII/HDAC/MEF2 pathway

Endothelin-1 / phenylalanine bind to the GPCR receptor on the cardiomyocyte

Activate G protein

G protein will activate phospholipase C (PLC)

PLC converts PIP2 into IP3 and DAG

IP3 travels to the nuclear membrane and bind to nearby sarcoplasmic reticulum

Release of Ca2+ → Increase Ca2+ flux into the nucleus

The high Ca2+ concentration activates Calmodulin Kinase II (CaMKII)

CaMKII phosphorylates histone deacetylase (HDAC)

HDAC travels outside of the nucleus, leading to the activation of myocyte enhancer factor 2 (MEF2)

→ Hypertrophic gene expression

→ Heart failure (Pathological, not physiological gene expression)

VASCULAR TONE

问Why is arterial diameter important for determining blood flow & blood pressure?
答

Because resistance is extremely sensitive to radius, according to Poiseuille’s law:

A small decrease in radius → HUGE increase in resistance

Resistance controls arterial pressure and organ perfusion

Thus vascular tone, by adjusting VSMC contraction and radius, is a major determinant of blood pressure.

问What mechanisms make the vascular smooth muscle cell contract?
答

Ca2+ enters the cell

Ca2+ binds to RyR → Releases more Ca2+

Ca2+ binds to Calmodulin (CaM)

Ca2+/CaM complex activates Myosin Light Chain Kinase (MLCK)

MLCK phosphorylates Regulatory Light Chain 20 (RLC20) on myosin heads → Allow for the myosin heads to bind to actin filaments

→ Start actin-myosin cross-bridge cycling

→ Muscle contraction (vasoconstriction)

问What mechanisms make the vascular smooth muscle cell relax?
答

Ca2+ leaves the cell / comes back to the SR

No more Ca2+/CaM complex → Deactivation of Myosin Light Chain Kinase (MLCK)

Myosin Light Chain Phosphatase (MLCP) is activated → Dephosphorylates RLC20

Myosin heads detach from actin filaments

→ Stop actin-myosin cross-bridge cycling

→ Muscle relaxation (vasodilation)

问What method can be used to monitor arterial tone ex vivo? What is the principle?
答

Isolate artery → mount between wires or pins

Stretched to resting tension in physiological buffer

Drugs are added: vasoconstrictors, vasodilators, …

The mechanical signals are recorded

→ Record tension vs. time

CARDIOVASCULAR PHARMACOLOGY

Therapeutic targets & mechanism of drugs acting on the sympathetic nervous system

β-blockers (β₁-selective preferred in CV disease):

Target: β₁-adrenergic receptors in the heart and kidney.

Mechanism: Blocks Gs → Reduce cAMP → Reduce PKA → Reduce Ca2+ entry into the cell

Result:

Reduces HR → Negative chronotropic effect

Reduces contractility → Negative inotropic effect

α₁-blockers

Target: α₁-adrenergic receptors on vascular smooth muscle

Mechanism: Block Gq → Downregulation of IP3 → Decrease Ca2+ concentration → vasodilation

α₂-agonists

Target: α₂-presynaptic receptors in CNS

Mechanism: Oppose the effect of the sympathetic nervous system → Decrease heart rate & blood pressure

Therapeutic targets & mechanism of drugs acting on the RAAS system

ACE inhibitors:

Target: Angiotensin-converting enzyme (conversion of Angiotensin I → Angiotensin II)

Mechanism: Inhibit the conversion of Ang I → Ang I → vasodilation

(Bradykinin accumulation → Coughing)

Angiotensin receptor blockers (ARBs)

Target: Angiotensin II receptor

Mechanism: competitive antagonism of the angiotensin II receptor → vasodilation, block aldosterone release

(Similar to ACE inhibitors but without bradykinin-related cough)

Renin inhibitors

Target: Rennin

Mechanism: Prevent the conversion of Angiotensinogen → Angiotensin I

Therapeutic targets & mechanisms of drugs acting on calcium channels in the heart & vessels

Phenylalkylamines (verapamil)

Target: CaV1.2 channels (open-state, rate-dependent)

Mechanism: Decrease calcium entry in the myocardium

Benzothiazepines (diltiazem) → similar effects

Dihydropyridine (nifedipine)

Target: CaV1.2 channels in inactivated state (voltage-dependent).

Mechanism: Potent vasodilation → Decrease BP

Mechanisms that decrease BP

Decrease cardiac output: beta-blockers, diuretics, Cardiac CCBs (verapamil/diltiazem)

Decrease peripheral vascular resistance: ACE inhibitors, ARBs → vasodilation

Mechanisms that increase oxygen supply to the myocardium

Beta blockers → Decrease heart rate → Increase diastolic filling time

Vasodilators (ACEs, ARBs) → Improve blood flow

Dihydropyridine CCBs → strong vasodilator → Improve blood flow

Mechanisms that diminish oxygen needs of the myocardium

Beta blockers → Lower HR → Lower oxygen demand

ACE inhibitors, ARBs → Vasodilation → Less wall tension → Less oxygen need

Cardiac CCBs (verapamil, diltiazem) → Decrease heart rate & contractility → Decrease O2 demand

VASCULAR FUNCTIONS

ENDOTHELIAL QUIESCENCE & ACTIVATION

The cardinal features of endothelial activation & how endothelial cells drive them

Pro-inflammatory

Pro-thrombotic

Permeable

How endothelial cells drive these features

Pro-inflammatory phenotype

Upon activation, endothelial cells upregulate adhesion molecules such as P-selectin, E-selectin, ICAM-1, VCAM-1

This drives the leukocyte recruitment cascade:

Rolling via P/E-selection

Firm adhesion via ICAM-1/VCAM-1

Diapedesis through junctions

Pro-thrombotic phenotype

Activated endothelial cells express and expose major procoagulant surfaces and molecules

Tissue Factor (TF) → Initiates coagulation

Von Willebrand Factor (vWF) & P-selectin → Platelet adhesion & aggregation

Phosphatidylserine (PtdSer) → accelerate coagulation reactions

Increased vascular permeability

Disruption of inter-endothelial junctions, especially VE-cadherin & tight junction complexes

This results in increased paracellular gaps, leakage of plasma proteins into tissues, promote inflammation

Endothelial adhesion molecules and their role

Selectins (P-selectin, E-selectin)

Role: mediate leukocyte rolling on the endothelial surface. Mediates leukocyte rolling on the endothelial surface. P-selectin is stored in Weibel-Palade bodies and rapidly mobilized upon activation. E-selectin is transcriptionally induced more slowly.

Immunoglobulin-like adhesion molecules

ICAM-1 (Intercellular Adhesion Molecule-1)

VCAM-1 (Vascular Cell Adhesion Molecule-1)

Role: Stabilize firm adhesion of leukocytes via integrin binding, allowing diapedesis

Weibel-Palade bodies (WPB)

  • Endothelial-specific storage granule, visible as elongated organelles
  • Serve as rapid-response secretory granules
  • Molecules contained in WPBs:
    • vWF: von Willebrand factor (abundant)
    • P-selectin
    • Angiopoietin-2
    • IL-8
    • Endothelin-1
    • And other pro-inflammatory & pro-thrombotic factors
  • Process of content release
    • Exocytosis
    • Activation stimuli (thrombin, histamine, TNF-α) trigger the fusion of WPBs with the plasma membrane
  • Effects on endothelial response:
    • vWF release → platelet adhesion & aggregation
    • P-selectin surface expression → leukocyte rolling
    • Cytokine release → recruitment of immune cells (pro-inflammatory & pro-thrombotic effects)

One endothelial receptor involved in maintaining quiescence:

  • Receptor: Tie2
  • Tie 2: endothelial receptor tyrosine kinase → maintaining vascular quiescence
  • Effects:
    • Anti-inflammatory
    • Anti-thrombotic
    • Endothelial barrier protective effect
    • Survival phenotype
  • Agonistic ligand:
    • Angiopoietin-1 (Ang-1)
    • Secreted by mural cells (vascular smooth muscle cells)
    • Ang-1 binding activates Tie2 to maintain vascular stability
  • Antagonistic ligand:
    • Angiopoietin-2 (Ang-1)
    • Stored in Weibel-Palade Bodies of endothelial cells
    • Released upon EC activation, Ang-2 competes with Ang-1 and inhibits Tie2

012025-10-10

Basic Immunology Take home-message 基础免疫学重点信息总结

1
免疫类型有哪些？ What are the different types of immunity? ● 包括固有免疫和适应性免疫。 ● Includes innate immunity and adaptive immunity.
2
推动这些免疫类型的细胞有哪些？ What are the types of cells that drive these different types of immunity? ● 固有免疫主要由粒细胞（如中性粒细胞、嗜酸性粒细胞）、单核细胞和部分天然淋巴细胞介导。 ● Innate immunity is mainly mediated by granulocytes (such as neutrophils, eosinophils), monocytes, and some innate lymphoid cells. ● 适应性免疫由淋巴细胞主导，尤其是T细胞和B细胞。 ● Adaptive immunity is driven primarily by lymphocytes, especially T cells and B cells.
3
免疫细胞如何沟通？ How do immune cells communicate? ● 细胞间通过分泌细胞因子（如1/2级细胞因子）、抗原提呈和细胞表面分子相互作用进行信号交流。 ● Cells communicate via secreting cytokines (such as 1st/2nd order cytokines), antigen presentation, and surface molecule interactions.
4
概括一个免疫反应的基本原理。 Give the general principal of an immune response. ● 固有免疫流程： 病原体 → 传感器/效应器 → 反应，或 病原体 → 传感器 → 一级细胞因子 → 效应器 → 反应。 ● Principle of innate immunity: Pathogen → Sensor/Effector → Response, or Pathogen → Sensor → 1st order cytokine → Effector → Response. ● 固有或适应性免疫流程（取决于淋巴细胞类型）： 病原体 → 传感器 → 一级细胞因子 → 淋巴细胞 → 二级细胞因子 → 效应器 → 反应。 ● Principle of innate or adaptive immunity (depending on lymphocyte type): Pathogen → Sensor → 1st order cytokine → Lymphocyte → 2nd order cytokine → Effector → Response.
老师补充内容
Teacher’s supplements: ● 适应性免疫依赖于淋巴细胞的抗原特异性重排故能产生针对特定抗原的高度靶向应答。 ● Adaptive immunity relies on antigen-specific receptor rearrangement in lymphocytes, allowing highly targeted responses against specific antigens. ● 固有淋巴样细胞和先天性免疫细胞主要通过产生大量细胞因子启动局部或系统免疫反应。 ● Innate lymphoid cells and other innate immune cells mainly initiate immune responses by producing large amounts of cytokines. ● 专业抗原提呈细胞（如树突状细胞）连接固有与适应性免疫，因其能高效保存并提呈免疫决定簇（表位），促进淋巴细胞激活。 ● Professional antigen-presenting cells (such as dendritic cells) bridge innate and adaptive immunity due to their ability to efficiently preserve and present immunodominant epitopes for lymphocyte activation. ● 免疫细胞沟通的核心是通过细胞因子和抗原提呈；其中细胞因子为信息传递的主要方式。 ● The core of immune cell communication is through cytokines and antigen presentation, with cytokines being the main medium of message delivery 1.

基础免疫学重点信息总结 Basic Immunology Take Home-Message

问1. 免疫有哪些类型？ What are the types of immunity?
答

免疫分为固有免疫和适应性免疫。 Immunity is divided into innate immunity and adaptive immunity.

固有免疫为第一道防线，非特异性识别。 Innate immunity acts as the first line of defense and recognizes pathogens non-specifically.

适应性免疫能针对特定抗原产生反应。 Adaptive immunity generates responses specific to particular antigens. 1

问2. 各类免疫由哪些细胞介导？ Which cells mediate the types of immunity?
答

固有免疫主要由粒细胞（如中性粒细胞、嗜酸性粒细胞）、单核细胞和部分固有淋巴细胞介导。 Innate immunity is mainly mediated by granulocytes (such as neutrophils, eosinophils), monocytes, and some innate lymphoid cells.

适应性免疫由淋巴细胞（T细胞和B细胞）主导。 Adaptive immunity is dominated by lymphocytes, mainly T and B cells.

固有淋巴样细胞（如innate lymphoid cell）是刚被发现的细胞，它们主要产生大量细胞因子，启动免疫反应。 Innate lymphoid cells are recently discovered and mainly produce large amounts of cytokines to initiate immune responses. 1

问3. 哪些细胞连接了固有与适应性免疫？ Which cells link innate and adaptive immunity?
答

专业抗原递呈细胞（如树突状细胞）连接固有免疫与适应性免疫。 Professional antigen-presenting cells (such as dendritic cells) bridge innate and adaptive immunity.

树突状细胞能够有效保存抗原表位，并进行表位递呈，从而激活T细胞。 Dendritic cells are able to preserve antigenic epitopes effectively and present them to activate T cells.

树突状细胞内的pH 更可控，因此能防止表位过度降解。 The pH in dendritic cells is more controlled, thus preventing excessive degradation of epitopes.

吞噬抗原后的溶酶体与内含体参与抗原降解和处理，被保存下来的表位展示在细胞表面。 After antigen phagocytosis, lysosomes and endosomes process and degrade antigens, and preserved epitopes are presented on the cell surface. 1

问4. 免疫细胞如何沟通？ How do immune cells communicate?
答

免疫细胞之间通过分泌细胞因子和抗原递呈等方式进行交流。 Immune cells communicate via secretion of cytokines and antigen presentation.

细胞因子是信息传递的主要方式。 Cytokines are the major means of information transfer. 1

问5. 免疫反应的基本原理是什么？ What is the general principle of an immune response?
答

固有免疫过程：病原体→传感器细胞→一级细胞因子→效应细胞→反应。 Process of innate immunity: pathogen → sensor cell → first order cytokine → effector cell → response.

适应性免疫过程：病原体→传感器细胞→一级细胞因子→淋巴细胞→二级细胞因子→效应细胞→反应。 Process of adaptive immunity: pathogen → sensor cell → first order cytokine → lymphocyte → second order cytokine → effector cell → response.

适应性免疫具有记忆性和特异性。 Adaptive immunity exhibits memory and specificity. 3

问6. 自身耐受的基本机制 What is the basic mechanism of self-tolerance?
答

免疫系统通过教育B细胞和T细胞防止自身免疫反应，这一过程分别在骨髓和胸腺发生。 The immune system educates B and T cells to prevent autoimmunity; this process occurs in the bone marrow and thymus respectively.

中枢和外周耐受共同参与自我耐受的维持。 Central and peripheral tolerance together maintain self-tolerance. 23

基础免疫学重点总结 Basic Immunology Take home-message

问1. 免疫有哪些不同类型？ What are the different types of immunity?
答

免疫分为固有免疫和适应性免疫。 Immunity includes innate immunity and adaptive immunity.

固有免疫是快速的、非特异性的防御；适应性免疫则针对特定抗原、有记忆性。 Innate immunity is rapid and non-specific, while adaptive immunity targets specific antigens and has memory. 2

问2. 这些免疫类型由哪些细胞负责？ What are the types of cells that drive these different types of immunity?
答

固有免疫由中性粒细胞、嗜酸性粒细胞、嗜碱性粒细胞、单核细胞以及固有淋巴样细胞负责。 Innate immunity is mediated by neutrophils, eosinophils, basophils, monocytes, and innate lymphoid cells.

适应性免疫主要由T细胞和B细胞驱动。 Adaptive immunity is mainly driven by T cells and B cells. 2

问3. 免疫细胞如何沟通？ How do the immune cells communicate?
答

免疫细胞通过分泌细胞因子（如IL-4、IL-5、IFN-γ等）、细胞间直接接触或者抗原递呈进行交流。 Immune cells communicate by secreting cytokines (such as IL-4, IL-5, IFN-γ), direct cell-to-cell contact, or antigen presentation.

细胞因子在免疫调节和效应功能中起核心作用。 Cytokines are central in immune regulation and effector functions. 2

4
概括一个免疫反应的基本原理。 Give the general principal of an immune response.

基本流程：病原体进入，传感器细胞识别抗原，产生一级细胞因子，招募或激活效应细胞，产生免疫反应。 Basic process: Pathogen entry, sensor cell recognition, production of first-order cytokines, recruitment or activation of effector cells, and generation of immune response.

若涉及淋巴细胞（T/B细胞），则进一步产生二级细胞因子，具体分化相应效应反应。 When lymphocytes (T/B cells) are involved, second-order cytokines are produced, leading to specialized effector responses. 1

问5. 什么是中枢耐受与外周耐受？ What is central tolerance vs peripheral tolerance?
答

中枢耐受：B细胞和T细胞在骨髓和胸腺“受教育”，对自身成分不反应。 Central tolerance: B and T cells are ‘educated’ in bone marrow and thymus not to react to self.

外周耐受：在外周淋巴器官（如脾脏、淋巴结）发生调节，进一步防止自身免疫。 Peripheral tolerance: Occurs in peripheral organs (spleen, lymph nodes), providing additional mechanisms to prevent autoimmunity.

问6. T细胞有哪些不同类型？
问What are the different types of T cells?
答

常见T细胞包括CD4+ T辅助细胞（如Th1、Th2、Th17、Treg）和CD8+细胞毒T细胞。 There are CD4+ helper T cells (such as Th1, Th2, Th17, Treg) and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells.

问7. B细胞的功能是什么？
问What does a B cell do?
答

B细胞的主要功能是产生抗体。分别可分化为产生IgM、IgG、IgE、IgA等不同类型抗体的浆细胞。 B cells mainly produce antibodies and can differentiate into plasma cells that secrete different isotypes: IgM, IgG, IgE, IgA.

补充—B细胞抗体分型及其功能（见右侧图）

Supplement—B cell antibody isotypes and their functions (see right diagram):

IgM：激活补体，初次免疫反应 Complement activation, primary immune response

IgG亚类：调理和吞噬，激活补体，胎盘转运 Fc-mediated phagocytosis, complement activation, neonatal immunity (placental transfer)

IgE：对抗蠕虫，肥大细胞脱颗粒，速发型过敏 Immunity against helminths, mast cell degranulation (immediate hypersensitivity)

IgA：黏膜免疫，跨上皮输送 Mucosal immunity (transport across epithelia)

心脏免疫学要点总结 Cardio-immunology Take home-message

问1. 心脏中最重要的免疫细胞是什么？
问What are the most important immune cells in the heart?
答

心脏中最重要的免疫细胞是组织驻留巨噬细胞。 The most important immune cells in the heart are resident macrophages.

老师强调，这些巨噬细胞不是“游走”过来的，而是长期定居在心脏组织中的常驻细胞。 The teacher emphasized that these macrophages are not migratory but are resident cells permanently settled in the heart tissue. 65

2
心脏驻留巨噬细胞的三大主要功能：
问What are the three main functions of resident macrophages in the heart?
答

1）清除细胞碎片，维持线粒体完整性。 (1) Clearing cellular debris and maintaining mitochondrial integrity.

巨噬细胞会识别和清除受损线粒体或凋亡后残留的碎片，帮助维持心肌细胞线粒体的完整和功能。 Macrophages identify and phagocytose damaged mitochondria or apoptotic debris, helping to preserve the integrity and function of cardiomyocyte mitochondria. 52

2）调节局部炎症，维护组织稳态。 (2) Regulating local inflammation and maintaining tissue homeostasis.

通过识别线粒体损伤信号（比如心磷脂等分子以及TLR9通路），巨噬细胞能精细调控炎症反应，避免过度炎症损伤心脏。 By sensing mitochondrial damage signals (such as cardiolipin and TLR9 pathway), macrophages finely regulate inflammation to avoid excessive inflammatory injury in the heart. 5

3）通过与心肌细胞形成缝隙连接，促进心脏电信号传导。 (3) Promoting cardiac electrical conduction by forming gap junctions with cardiomyocytes.

巨噬细胞能与心肌细胞建立“缝隙连接（gap junction）”，实现离子和信号的交流，保证心脏正常兴奋传导和收缩。 Macrophages can form "gap junctions" with cardiomyocytes, allowing the exchange of ions and signals to ensure proper cardiac excitation-conduction and contraction. 5

3
其它课堂重点补充：
